In fact, you get correlation all the way up kind of to high degrees, and therefore we have some hope of theory getting us through it.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Resolution, singular learning theory and capabilities externalities</span></h2><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=548"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So I don't know if we should… Whether or not it's wise for us to spend a lot of time talking about this other sector, but to change sectors: Although I'm still currently on the whole net positive on Resolution I think there's— Like, I look at something like </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/singular-learning-theory"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">singular learning theory</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and I'm like, "Gosh, that sure seems more capabilities than alignment to me."</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=574"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So if you're trying to do kind of modeling of bounded rationality so that you understand how to model superintelligence, so that you can then do kind of alignment training schemes kind of based on that understanding, I think there's some deep need to have a model of heuristics and how they are learned and kind of what kinds of heuristics are learned. It might not be required, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Irving indicates low disagreement]</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> but I'm like very sure that trans—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=708"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Oh, gotcha yeah.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=709"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah. Transformers are n— Yeah. So—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=711"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I would claim the future ASI totally just fits on this hardware. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[holds up his smartphone] [Yudkowsky indicates very low disagreement]</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[laughter]</span></i></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=720"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah. So I do have some worry that when you do things that look a little more dual use or capability flavored, that they are feeding that much more into capabilities and your alignment team is very close to the bottom of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cpdsMuAHSWhWnKdog/security-mindset-and-the-logistic-success-curve"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">logistic success curve</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. So you're moving us noticeably closer to the end of the world on capabilities and not very much closer to the saving of the world on alignment. Anytime you study something that's more like 50–50 than like 20–80 on capabilities–alignment.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=756"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah. So I guess maybe one organizational note is that there are people that have… I try to say this in a way that doesn't reveal who the person is, but I have decided to not work with people recently because they're not doing kind of careful analysis of how to be differential here.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think it is… I basically agree with you that it is hard to do any kind of understanding of heuristics and generalization that doesn't have some degree of capability externality.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think I don't agree that if you're trying to do it in a way— I think there's a question of how much of the capabilities are coming out of hill climbing and tuning and data and kind of the mechanics that are being kind of run at AI labs. And I place just a ton of the speed is happening due to those factors. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Yudkowsky indicates very low disagreement]</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> And I think we will… Therefore, I expect that the theoretical work is like the default, even though you're intervening on— there's an easier dimension you're intervening on.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But I think that because alignment is going so far behind, I do think that the net effect is most likely positive, but not without a risk of being negative.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=850"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So I have updated enormously towards AI capabilities progress coming out of people doing alchemy rather than—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=863"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Right.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=863"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> You know,—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=867"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Thinking carefully.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=870"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Heuristic-based search as opposed to—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=872"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Sure—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=873"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Like poking-at-things-based search. That's too severe. A bunch of the poking at things is very much heuristic. Like they're not trying random learning schedules, they're trying cosines because computationally simpler learning schedules might be more likely to work than completely random ones. And yet—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=894"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I don't think that's the reason they chose cosine. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[laughter]</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> That's an unimportant disagreement to get into.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=898"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah. Well, it's easy to exaggerate this viewpoint, and I have to work myself not to parody it when I speak it. That said, my massive update towards how much AI progress would be driven by alchemy came with an accompanying cratering of my estimate of how good human institutions would be at the sort of basic progress that could lead to alignment.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=925"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Sure.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=925"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> And I think if you unblock the sort of basic progress that leads to alignment, you're thereby also unblocking the sort of basic progress that leads to doing stuff less alchemically. Like, the reason it's all alchemy is not that alignment research wouldn't contribute to capabilities. It's that humans are so bad at alignment research that it contributes very little to capabilities. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Yudkowsky laughs]</span></i></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Should anyone do alignment research</span></h2><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=954"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think this is a reasonable view. I think there are kind of two ways to go. So there's kind of two natural conclusions from this.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One is that, and this is certainly a thing people have kind of proposed to me, that I should not do any alignment research because— well, there are two arguments there. One argument is that it will cause people to not be so worried about things going wrong and so then they'll plow ahead. And the other one is, I think the thing you're saying about, well, just better understanding will give us kind of more speed up.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think, and then the other… But I find it hard to believe, kind of just in terms of basic heuristics mentally, that that should mean you shouldn't try to solve the problem. So why do I think this? I think this possibly does come back to how big a delta do you expect in the training pipeline required to cause a solution to work out?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If it's like, well, you have some combination of </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/claude-character"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">character training</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> plus kind of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/scalable-oversight"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">scalable oversight</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, plus some form of interpretability and there's a version of that recipe that works, and it's not kind of completely far away from what we have now, then I think in that world, there's a potential for you to make some understanding of kind of general agent heuristics.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It gives you a lens on what classes of things kind of work and don't work. You sort of hand that off to lab alignment teams. They try things. It sort of replicates. You have a combination now of kind of theoretical arguments and kind of empirical validation at low scale, low relative to ASI now. And kind of that gives you a story which you're not all that confident in the answer, but it's better, much better than we have now.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And the question is, like— Somehow that feels like to me it happens prior to causing the paradigm shift in capability research to switch from heuristic poking at things to more principled searching.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1098"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I'm not sure we should dive into this depth first yet, but I have some theoretical doubts about </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/persona-selection-model"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">persona selection</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and about scalable oversight, and about interpretability continuing to hold up and letting you fix the things that you find with interpretability.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mech interp and alien concepts</span></h2><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1119"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So for interpretability, I don't yet have a plan to have a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/interpretability-ml-and-ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">mech interp</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> team at Resolution. I was pitching that earlier today. Maybe we will in the future if it's like sufficiently different, but we can skip that one at least.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1130"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The mech interp teams elsewhere are much less terrible than the poking at it until it misbehaves at alignment and talking about scalable things, teams other places. Like, I do feel there's a legit sense in which that is not the primary place that needs your effort. Like, mech interp has always been healthier than the rest of the field.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1150"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think talking about personas and scalable oversight as specific topics does seem useful to me. Which one would you prefer to do first, if so?</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1158"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> With the side note that my basic objection to mech interp is visibility disappears in the limit, and just because you can see something going wrong doesn't mean you can fix it.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1168"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Maybe it's worth saying that the two issues I have with mech interp currently is that we've tried it for a long time, and we've gotten kind of not that much success.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1177"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1177"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> And then the other one is that, necessarily, ASIs will have crazy alien heuristics. And so the only sense in which you could interpret it would be you have some access to its alien concepts, and then you know the… You understand the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Yudkowsky indicates medium disagreement]</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> expansion process to pull out those concepts into a form that is way, way, way larger than a human could understand. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Yudkowsky indicates medium disagreement]</span></i></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1199"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah. I bet if they weren't trying to encrypt that, there'd be a bunch of stuff in there that corresponds to things that you can see in the world.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1206"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> To be clear, if they can try to encrypt it, you're totally wrecked. That's </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2204.06974"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a theorem by Vinod [Vaikuntanathan] and Or Zamir</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1207"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah. Sure. I expect mech interp to break down more because they know we're looking and less because the natural compressed form has zero things in it that map one to one to the ways that humans compress the universe, if that makes sense.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1230"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Sure. I think that's a place where we do disagree, but it probably is a bit hard. I think there's some, like… I have sort of some computational sort of sufficiency model of the world where a sufficiently long computation can in fact… Like, humans can eventually understand most things, but eventually is, like, much longer than the age of the universe. If you just didn't train somebody, you just let them play out the reasoning.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And then if you want to compress things down into some compact form that you can run with only a billion tokens, then you need these crazy alien concepts. But that under this sort of computational, eventually, the enough computation or enough is, like, ridiculous, would be enough, then the process is know how to expand from kind of alien things upwards to much larger traces.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1281"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> It's a pity that this probably isn't our most important disagreement because I bet it would be one of the easiest to make progress on. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Yudkowsky laughs]</span></i></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1287"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Well, this is the scalable oversight one in some sense.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1289"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Oh.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1290"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> It actually… maybe this is like… yeah, we can choose to go there.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1294"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But I'm the one who's more optimistic here, so—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1296"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> About what?</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1296"><b><span natural way of parsing up the universe would have a bunch of one-to-one correspondences to the way that humans parse up the universe.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1307"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Oh, you think it would have that correspondence?</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1308"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1308"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span
