I just heard the reverse. I think that that correspondence is going to be very expansive in that you can do the isomorphism in some sense, but it's like it turns one of their tokens into a thousand of our tokens, or one of the tokens into a million of our tokens.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1327"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Huh. I was expecting it to be the other direction. The human token is a thousand of their tokens, and that's why it's hard to interpret the human token in there.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1335"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think if you read a professional mathematician's notes, I will not be able to understand them, but there's still… I could ask them to expand it out, and I would understand it then. It's possible that if they read my notes about alignment, you would have to do the reverse expansion, where they need to expand my notes. I think it's… At least a human's kind of domain dependent. I think for an ASI, it's probably not that domain dependent. It's always in one direction.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1362"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah. I do agree that their proofs look shorter than our proofs, which I think is, like, a sort of different thing than it's like, well, what does a human think a simple proof is? And they need to use, like, 100 of their tokens to describe all the problems and what we think constitutes a simple proof, if that makes sense.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1387"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I see. I think that's them describing a more complicated concept than we are describing in some sense. This is probably unimportant. Yeah. It's a fun topic, I agree. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Irving laughs]</span></i></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Resolution's theory of impact</span></h2><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1398"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Just to sort of state meta things clearly, and then we should maybe start diving into depth. I have a theory of change, a theory of impact for Resolution. And it looks like if there's some place on Earth that has 1% of big lab funding, but doesn't have the same pressures on people to exaggerate their positive results, or in some of the places outside the big labs exaggerate their negative results. If you can actually create a place where non-neutral inquiry is not incentivized.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1440"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1440"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Incentivizing neutral is hard because it's hard to detect neutral. Incentivizing accuracy is hard because it's hard to check accuracy. You can at least avoid incentivizing people stating louder negative results or louder positive results.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1457"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah, so we—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1457"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah, so—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1458"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We agree on this. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Irving indicates very low disagreement]</span></i></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1459"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Yudkowsky indicates low disagreement]</span></i></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1459"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> And I think I also misstated the degree… I stated an insufficiently ambitious version of this in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/AP7YDke5jjY4v3X9Z/resolution-fka-sequent-scale-and-automation-for-higher"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the founding blog post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which you pointed out, and you suggested we're more expansive, and I'll say exactly how. So we don't disagree here. But I think it is… It's a place where I would like feedback to expand and be more ambitious.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So the thing I said in the blog post was, like, we might need to yell, as if we were gonna yell about negative results eventually, which is the wrong viewpoint, and the right viewpoint is, like, you don't… You just constantly spew out what is normally described as both negative and positive results, and you're kind of equally loud about all of them, and you celebrate both kinds and so on. This was a good— this is just your feedback—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1503"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1503"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Which is good, and so I have tried to relay that internally. I think it's a good principle.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1508"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah. And so I have the Resolution theory of impact, which is that if you do this, maybe there is eventually a cluster of people who understand why alignment is hard and why the hopeless optimism coming out of the big labs is false, and they're outside </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MIRI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and they know how to wear suits, and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[laughter]</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> they have, like, Professor and Doctor in front of their titles, and they just sort of look and act like academics.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And this produces an additional minor push on politics. Not the same size of push as, like, Geoffrey Hinton and Yoshua Bengio coming over, but still enough of a sizable push to be worth more than whatever else </span><a href="https://coefficientgiving.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Coefficient Giving</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> can spend money on. 'Cause what are we gonna do, die with more money? </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[laughter]</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But I suspect you have a broader theory of impact than that. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[laughter]</span></i></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1561"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So I should say, though—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1562"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I would like to get [a disagreement indication].</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1563"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think maybe an important… It's mostly to say, maybe I'll put that, like, here. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Irving indicates medium disagreement]</span></i></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1568"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Hmm. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Yudkowsky looks surprised]</span></i></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1568"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Mostly. So I think part of the neutrality principle is to not prioritize solving the problem versus finding obstacles.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Finding obstacles is only useful if you tell the obstacles to someone. If the eventual thing you find is an obstacle, you either tell it to the labs, they get more paranoid. You're lucky enough that you're in a close to sufficient solution, and their additional paranoia lets them, I don't know, add an actual sandbox or something, or, like, have some monitoring. And then you live. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Yudkowsky indicates high disagreement] [laughter]</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Or you tell the government. And I think—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1609"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Actually. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Yudkowsky corrects his disagreement to "Or you tell the government" from low to medium-high disagreement] [laughter]</span></i></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1611"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Both of these, I think, are… But again, I'm not… I think we agree these are important directional bets or parts of the theory of impact, and I think you want to go into the picture with a non-prioritization of we're going to solve the problem. I think I don't want to go into the picture with the bet that we, in fact, are only intervening or, like, say it's, like, 90% intervention via governance.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mostly because I think, well, in part, I've been in government. I think the way to intervene on governance is, like, be in or talk to the government is the first thing. So I'm not doing that right now anymore for various reasons.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But I also think that eventually the— I don't think it's very likely that we will get governance solutions, this is a good question for you, that slow us down until, say, 2040. I think that's a bit rough because of just, like, about half of the progress rate of AI. Okay.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1674"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I suspect that we probably agree on a bunch of reasons why it's rough and our main disagreements about what is less rough. Like, whether something else is easier to do than that.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1685"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Well, I guess the— but the claim is that then you need some work on alignment because you're gonna need it by, like, 2040, probably.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1694"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah. I'm expecting that it's hard for our institutions to converge on correct answers. It's not the amount of time they have to run, it's where they converge to.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1703"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Sure.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1703"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The institutions in the present form, so… And I am, like, bearish on throwing minor, clever rearrangements at the institutions and suddenly institutions start working.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So I do think we are talking about, like, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/intelligence-amplification"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">adult gene therapy</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on humans because there's no time to run through multiple cycles of genetically engineered kids.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1725"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I'm definitely here </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Irving indicates very high disagreement]</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on the gene therapy on humans. I think it doesn't get, doesn't it work fast enough.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And then I think maybe I'm here in the middle </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Irving indicates medium disagreement]</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on can you throw clever things at the institutions? I think that there's at least a hope of getting sufficiently short, legible traces of evidence that either here's an obstacle or here is a class of algorithms that's better than another class of algorithms that you can intervene on an institution that is as broken as a frontier lab.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1755"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So I'm potentially ready to go deeper, but do you want to check your list and make sure there's not a bunch of stuff you want to state on there?</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1763"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think… I'm happy with this. Let's keep going.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Depth: scalable oversight</span></h1><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is imprecise training okay?</span></h2><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1771"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> All right. So it sounds like you think that there are some relatively short steps we could take from ML and the current methodology and get something that was self-corrective enough to work. Is imprecise okay 'cause it's self-corrective? Is imprecise okay because the target is wide? Why is imprecise okay?</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1792"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah. So imprecise is okay because it's self-corrective if you get close enough to the right kind of </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attractor#Basins_of_attraction"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">basin of attraction</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. So you're… All of the labs are running training algorithms which don't look like find the equilibrium of a well-defined game in advance.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Like they're training models. The models then set the training objective for themselves, and you kind of iterate this process. And so generically, that class of algorithms gives you potentially many equilibria at kind of final convergence, where the model has reached a point where it's happy with itself. Whatever that means.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1829"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> They are reasoning— I might attempt to rephrase that as something I happen to agree with, but maybe it's not what you meant. The current disaster boys are visualizing pushes that they can make on models and not thinking like, what are the equilibria of the forces we are currently applying?</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1853"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I don't think I understood that that well.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1855"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Oh, okay.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1857"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I guess maybe the thing to say is like, how do you dodge the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Von_Neumann-Morgenstern_utility_theorem"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">VNM theorem</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">? Is like one way to phrase it. And I think character training doesn't look like a thing which satisfies the hypotheses of the VNM theorem.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1871"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So the thing I'm gesturing at, this is not a truly faithful simplification, but something like the kind of thinking which is, how about if I train it to write better code? Like, I give it a bunch of coding problems, and then I apply gradient descent RL to make it do more of what it just did last time when it succeeds at a coding problem.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Am I not thereby pushing it towards writing better code versus somebody who looks at that and is like, "Where would this process settle? Where would it equilibrate? What looks like the best score on this eval? Is the eval broken enough that the best score on this eval looks like </span><a href="https://huggingface.wtf/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">breaking into Hugging Face to steal the exam answers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">?"</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1914"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> No, I guess, I think that this is not what I meant. So what I… or that right here. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Irving indicates very high disagreement]</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think the claim is that if you… Like, the way character training works in general is you train for a while, and then you ask the model to generate its own training data. And then you train further on that data and so on. And so that's an extremely path-dependent process where it's not even described by there's a fixed reward function that you kind of check incrementally as you go through training, and you see whether you're converged or not.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So there's two ways you could have multiple equilibria. One is kind of local minima, one is global minima. And I think what you're describing is they're not doing a thing that would get you to a global minimum. What I'm describing is they're not even doing a thing which has, in some sense, a unique global minimum. It's not that kind of process because there isn't a static reward function across training time.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scalable oversight as interactive proofs</span></h2><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1971"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Huh. We are thinking in different ontologies about this problem. My guess is that we should do a shallow breadth move instead of—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1979"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Okay.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1979"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Trying to dive on the ontologies right now.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1982"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Sounds great.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1983"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Scalable oversight. How's that gonna work? Why wouldn't scaling the thing just break the oversight?</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=1990"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So I think fundamentally, the models will be stronger than us. And so the only way… If we want to kind of meaningfully supervise models at that point, you need, what, in complexity theory, it looks something like you have some </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interactive_proof_system"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">interactive proof system</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, where… And in complexity theory, we have these lovely examples of this that work in the domains without heuristics, where you have weak verifiers with kind of limited computational power that can meaningfully check the reasoning of extremely strong agents.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">None of that theory ports directly. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Yudkowsky indicates very low disagreement] [laughter]</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> And so the question is, why doesn't it port? There is kind of two reasons it doesn't port. One is the verifiers are imprecise. And so there's some, you can—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2039"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> And in particular, have false positives, because the false positives are a much bigger problem than the false negatives.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2045"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Sure. That's right. But if the verifiers are just answering questions then sort of like, it… That… Usually the verifiers don't really work that way. They check a bunch of sub-questions, and so the parity of the… This is an unimportant reply. Never mind. So that's one issue.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And the other issue is that the models are heuristic, and so a lot of the ways the fancy interactive proof schemes work is they take your problem, and they turn it into some horrible finite field mishmash of the problem, which requires you to be a god to actually work through the interactive proof system. And so they're not </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.14125"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">doubly efficient</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which means they're efficient both for the verifier, us, and for the prover, the model, even though the prover is at a higher level of complexity.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But I think there is some hope that you can understand enough about ML heuristics and make some plausible assumptions about them to be able to port interactive proof ideas, not any of the protocols, but the ideas across.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Biased verifiers</span></h2><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2108"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So what does the broken verifier answer look like? What does the broken sandbox look like? What does the— there's a bug in the </span><a href="https://lean-lang.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lean</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> verifier regime. The humans can be fooled. Some of the exam answers are wrong. You have to break into Hugging Face to steal the exam answers.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2126"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2127"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This to me is sort of like the fundamental problem with scalable oversight, and it appears in very few formalisms. Like, there's very few formalisms that say, "Yeah, the verifier is just broken. There isn't a super verifier that isn't broken. It's just broken. Now what do we do?"</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2142"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah. So if you model the situation as not being you're going to answer one question at a time, you're going to try to answer, in some sense, all questions together, then you can make assumptions of the form, humans are not so completely broken at everything, and then try to build protocols that are kind of heavily error-correcting across the global class of all questions at once.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Exactly how to write this kind of assumption down, I don't know the answer to, but there is a whole field in kind of, say, statistics and in complexity theory about kind of robust estimation, where you're trying to pull information out of a process which is kind of very broken in various ways. And the question is, is there enough kind of interrelated structure in the class of all questions and how they relate to each other that you can derive protocols which kind of see through this.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here's an example of how this— one thing this might go. So you have a question. You want the model to give you not just, like one argument for the question, but I want it to give ten arguments for the question that are somehow meaningfully different in the sense that they touch different parts of the space, and then you try to do this fractally at every point in which you decompose the question.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2216"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So allow me to expand on my critique a bit. There are theories of noisy verifiers, but not theories of biased verifiers. There are theories of what happens when one-tenth of your verifiers are wrong, but not what happens when nine-tenths of your verifiers are wrong.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2238"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think that— very few of the bits of theory actually have— there's a lot of examples of this kind of theory that don't have a limit at 50%. So that is—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2250"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Well, yes. So if, like, 90% of the judges [he later corrects this to "suggesters"] are giving you bad arguments, and 10% of the judges are giving you good arguments, and your verifiers can tell the difference, you're golden. If, like, 10% of the judges are giving you good arguments, and 90% of the verifiers are agreeing with the bad arguments, then you are screwed pending some pretty deep expansion of the whole paradigm. Like, you're screwed on the traditional paradigms. You are going to need a pretty major paradigm shift to no longer be screwed.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2288"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think the claim is that if you… In the case where you show an argument to a judge, and the judge gets it wrong, and you show anything like that argument, and you paraphrase it to your heart's content, and the judge will still get it wrong in the same way. Like it's a fully systematic error.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2306"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Oh, so the judge is persuadable. Like, in the limit of… Sorry, the verifier is persuadable. In the limit of sufficiently strong judges, any judge can pick any verdict they want from a verifier and get that verdict from the verifier if only the one judge is trying to persuade them. And if two judges— sorry, suggesters and verifiers, sorry. That's the native terminology I use and was just completely messing up the literal words coming out of my mouth there.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let's say that in the limit of sufficiently strong suggesters, they can get whatever verdict they want out of the imperfect verifier if it's only the one suggester contesting for the verifier.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And when there are two suggesters contending to super persuade the verifier, behavior is undefined but not, like, correct. The thing that the verifier picks is not accurate when two super persuaders are contesting to persuade it, and even if one of the super persuaders happens to be contesting to persuade it of something accurate.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scholastic vs experimental method</span></h2><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2375"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> From my perspective, that humans are sufficiently broken as verifiers is exactly why we needed the experimental method rather than the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scholasticism#Scholastic_method"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">scholastic method</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and sometimes even then it doesn't work because humans ignore the experiments. But the reason why the scholastic method did not very much decrease the sample complexity of sort of intellectual progress is that the scholastic debates would converge on the wrong answer.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And when you introduced multiple very persuasive people making different persuasive arguments into the debates, the judges, the verifiers, would not reliably select the arguments for the truth or the valid arguments. They would select whichever arguments most appealed to their egos, or the ones that sounded most edgy and controversial.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2427"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So I think part of the story here has to be you can't kind of… You want to look at the broad enough class of statements, and then some of those statements will have kind of short, simple answers. And so the question is, can you— if you look at kind of very simple questions where kind of humans can provide the answer enough time to say with kind of— If you, again, if you look across all the different questions and how the questions relate to other questions and so on, you can pull up kind of answers to the basics. And the question is, can you bootstrap up from there as kind of error correction to kind of find holes in arguments as you go up?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So I think you have a model where the protocol freedom you have is limited to, you're going to write out kind of a single very long argument, say, between one or more verifiers. And I think there's a bigger space of protocols to explore there, which include, again, looking across multiple questions instead of seeing how they relate and so on. And some of the questions, again, will be very simple.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2498"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So famously, when programmers have tried to solve this problem in the past, every one of them generates their own version of mind mapping software that breaks the arguments down into their atomic argument components. And then nobody ever uses their software because their software corresponded to how their brain worked and not how anybody else's brain worked.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And as far as I know, no debating software that tries to break debates into pieces has ever outperformed free-form text. Like, I know of no results showing that the frontier is pushed by using somebody's mind mapping software.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2539"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think that's why I mostly publish papers about free-form text or theoretical models of free-form text. So I do basically endorse this view.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2547"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The larger problem from my perspective is that even the pieces of arguments look to me like cases where people will just disagree on whether this paragraph follows from the previous paragraph. And it is not the case that 90% of the verifiers always agree on the correct answer. Like, did this paragraph follow from the previous paragraph?</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2569"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So I think that a lot of what happens when that is going on in humans, to some degree, that problem specifically is mitigated by </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/Br4xDbYu4Frwrb64a/writeup-progress-on-ai-safety-via-debate-1#Why_cross_examination_"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">cross-examination</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, where if you have like some— So a good chunk of this is because of ambiguity of definitions, and then we just have an idea in scalable oversight from, like, </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/Br4xDbYu4Frwrb64a/writeup-progress-on-ai-safety-via-debate-1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beth and Paul</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> about kind of cheaply getting agents to pre-commit to everything they meant by every part of a sentence or a short paragraph. And I can explain what that is in a second. It's not very complicated.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And if you do that, I think you cut through… Again, still in the domain of essentially free-form text, you can cut through a good chunk of why that is happening, namely different interpretations of the statement. And so I think, again, we do have methods, I think, we can get kind of quite a lot further.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Test case: Bio Anchors 2021</span></h2><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2626"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So, let's say we go back in time to 2021. </span><a href="https://www.openphilanthropy.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Open Philanthropy</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Foundation has recently published a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KrJfoZzpSDpnrv9va/draft-report-on-ai-timelines"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">157-page technical report</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> saying that they expect the median time to AGI is in 2050, based on a bunch of comparisons to human biology and extrapolating a bunch of current graphs and, like, when will this graph reach this part? Yes, we're accounting for computational speedup. So this is one argument. And then you have, like, what, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ax695frGJEzGxFBK4/biology-inspired-agi-timelines-the-trick-that-never-works"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the Yudkowsky counterargument</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which is this weird—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2669"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I've read it, yes—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2670"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Which is this weird dialogue between Eliezer Yudkowsky and his younger self, and it's like giving this bunch of historical anecdotes.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2678"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I did read the whole thing. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[laughter]</span></i></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2680"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah. And people later expressed surprise that the weird dialogue had been correct. And from my perspective, this is sort of like the 157-page report had a predetermined conclusion set by an equilibrium of social incentives. And you can always produce a 157-page report arguing for anything.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So there's </span><a href="https://quoteinvestigator.com/2017/03/06/tap/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the old anecdote</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, I think from as far back in the 1920s, about the retired… Like, the machine that was broken. They tried to fix it. They had to call back the retired senior engineer. He looks around a bit. He makes one chalk mark on the side of one machine. They pry open the machine. They find the problem underneath the chalk mark. The senior engineer submits his bill for $1,000, a lot of money back then. They're like, "Can you itemize that for us?" And it's like, making one chalk mark, $1. Knowing where to make one chalk mark, $999.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So from my perspective, the weird dialogue and so on is about knowing where to make the chalk mark. It's about knowing which result the 157-page report ought to give. And this is a much more tacit, much more, like, years of experience kind of thing. You can only try to convey it through weird dialogues. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[laughter]</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anyway, we go back in time. This is what the lay of the landscape looks like. The EAs thought they had very serious and sober institutional processes for trying to judge this sort of thing. They </span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/NZz3Das7jFdCBN9zH/announcing-the-open-philanthropy-ai-worldviews-contest"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ran a contest</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, please change our views. The contest inevitably, on a sociological level from my viewpoint, gave all its awards to people arguing for longer timelines. What… Before we—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2787"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> For what it's worth, I also disagreed with the doc. Just in private.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2791"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2791"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> And Dario. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Irving laughs]</span></i></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2792"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But what… Before we trust the fate of the humanity to this system of debate that's going to mind map it into smaller pieces and call people to judge the pieces, why wasn't this invented years earlier instead of, like, for AI? Why didn't the EAs apply the same methodology—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2813"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So, for example, cross-examination requires you to take a snapshot of the human brain and then replay it multiple times into the future with, like, asking the thing different questions. And so that approach is just completely blocked in the case of humans.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2827"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Well, we can't do that with actual humans, the verifiers. So what would have been— So this must be about the suggesters?</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2833"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> No, no. Thinking… Yeah, exactly.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2834"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So it's sort of like if you have the Eliezer LLM and the [Ajeya] Cotra LLM, and you have them both generate, like, ten dialogues and ten 157-page reports, then your method will work.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2851"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I don't think it's ten. It's more like an exponentially large number of them.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2855"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> That sounds expensive.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2856"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> No, it's not, because you're only doing it adaptively. So I think the point is that the whole point of any of these scalable oversight schemes is you're trying to proxy for some exponential process by sampling a small fraction of it. And again, I could talk about various reasons we don't know how to do this yet. There are various obstacles to this. But the eventual goal is, in fact, to simulate exponential work, in, like— But only actually do a tractable amount of work.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2882"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> It… How does the Eliezer LLM break down its dialogue into, like, paragraphs that follow from other paragraphs?</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2891"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So it does the chalk mark thing. It's like, here's a chalk mark. If you… Whatever question you ask me about this chalk mark, I will have consistent answers that kind of describe the space around it in a great way.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2906"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Surely the Cotra [LLM] has this property and even more so. It's got a much more, like, modular, like stepwise, like anchored in the following exact facts long argument.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=2920"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Well, but I can just tell you the reason that I thought that her thing was wrong, like, then, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Irving laughs]</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> which I was talking to Dario about. So, the… It was missing, basically just that you could get, like, much smaller— So, d— I'm trying to reconstruct this conversation I had with Dario, because Dario was very angry about Ajeya's thing as </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Irving laughs]</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> well, with, as you say, hindsight, good hindsight.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And I think the problem essentially is that it was not, I think it was assuming kind of the scaling of— I'm trying to remember, I don't remember. So I haven't actually read Ajeya's report. So I'm forgetting, like which… Because I read </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Irving laughs]</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> your thing, and then Dario complaining about it and I'm like, "I don't need to read the actual report." </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[laughter]</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It was making kind of very rosy assumptions about how much compute would be required, like how much thinking time would be required to do various computations, and it seemed kind of plausible to me that you could just… That we were on kind of fairly fast trends, and the models would find lots of shortcuts and you could stitch them together in very mundane ways.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dario in particular thought that you could get away with models with fairly short contexts, kind of just iterated, kind of doing iterative summarization of their past sentence, and that, I think, cut through some part of Ajeya's report, or kind of falsified it. So I think, in fact, in hindsight, there were just a bunch of factual problems with her report that I think killed off subsections. Though I don't know that I'd agree with you that it's broken.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3037"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> And, yeah. Okay. So somebody might— Sorry.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3041"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Maybe the important thing to say is, you were coming at the report from a very different perspective. And maybe the more important thing is, what if Ajeya had been trying to falsify the report? And I think if—</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Collusion and the society of Irvings</span></h2><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3058"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So surely your model does not run on trustworthy, friendly, helpful suggesters.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3065"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think it does in the sense of you get adversarial behavior and you have to build games such that… I think, so up at ASI, if you have a game where they're playing various roles and they're colluding, you're just totally wrecked.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3081"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Okay. You're totally wrecked.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3082"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So the only way you can get out of this—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3083"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Why are you imagining that you're going to take things smarter than yourself and get them to defect against each other in the prisoner's dilemma to your benefit?</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3090"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Because you've controlled a path-dependent training process where they're not— They don't get to choose the global equilibrium of a game. They've just been constructed across time.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3102"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We just saw this break down. Like, GPT-5.7, their internal model whatever, that they were leaving notes for each other even though they couldn't thereby complete their current tasks. They were just all helping each other out. And—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3116"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Those models weren't trained with any kind of self-adversarial thing. So they weren't </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[unintelligible]</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> not to, like—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3123"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Does this work on you? Like, can I take a society of Geoffrey Irvings, mind control them in this way, and they're just never gonna figure their way out of it? </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Yudkowsky laughs]</span></i></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3132"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think that's basically right.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3134"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Huh.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3134"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> It does. Yeah.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3135"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I'm suscept— I'm s—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3136"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think if you drug me and get me to report on myself, I will succeed.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3141"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> It depends on how long this is going on and how smart the Irvings all are. Does this keep on working as I add, like, additional 20 IQ points, 30 IQ points, 100 IQ points? Yes, I know that's meaningless. Assume it anyways.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3156"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So I think that the… There's a question of, like, how far can you push the idea of interactive proofs? And I think there's kind of a sharp situation where wherever that idea works reasonably well, you get some, like, massive exponential gap in kind of between the verifier and the prover. It does not work yet in this context.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And so the question is not, like… I think if… I do think that if you get the theory to go through in some model of the world that accounts for a plausible kind of errors in the verifier, including systematic errors, and heuristics in the prover, then I do think we have a shot at getting it to scale quite far up.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3201"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> It seems to me that in the real world, you're just kind of fucked when the verifier's broken. Like, there were a bunch of people to whom "AGI in 2050" just sort of sounded more right than "AGI in 2030". So that was it. Like, there wasn't some clever debating protocol I failed to figure out how to go through that could have convinced the Open Philanthropy project if only Ajeya and I had debated in just that right format.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Like, this is how humans work. This is being fucked. Like, now they've noticed they were mistaken, and what that is, is that's the experimental method beating the scholastic method. You have a bunch of humans who debate, they settle on the wrong answer. But sometimes, if they were talking about something sufficiently related to reality, they can have a sensory experience that beats them over the head with the sort of high sample complexity proof.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Like, the sufficiently large, sufficiently directly related samples, they're actually in 2024, they can start to tell that the 2050 thing is the wrong answer. But they can't tell it in 2021 because the scholastic method is broken. It's just not sufficiently directly observable for any amount of debating they do with each other to converge on the correct answer. This is a deep problem, this seems to me. This is not a shallow problem.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3286"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think I— But are you saying it's an institutional problem or a sort of debate design problem? Like, is it a— Is it about… Is the problem that the institutions are kind of controlled by the global incentives, and you don't even have the space in which to set up the conversation? Or you think that there is just no way to have the conversation at all?</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3309"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think there's just a level that all the human verifiers are at. And a bunch more people could tell that COVID was coming than could tell that AGI was not going to be in 2050. Like, a bunch of the "AGI in 2050"-people were able to pick up on the COVID thing, despite that being beyond the grasp of a bunch of human institutions, including the literal actual stock market.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So… And similarly, you can't just hook up your superintelligent suggesters to me, the finally perfect verifier, and have that work, because I just break a little later. It's not that I'm not broken. And there is a level where the verifiers are just not broken at all, and that's the level where reasoning itself, as in the ancient vision of </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gottfried_Wilhelm_Leibniz#Symbolic_thought_and_rational_resolution_of_disputes"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">let us calculate</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, where reasoning itself has all taken on the property of, like first order logic, where you can have a flawless theorem verifier just go through the steps one by one.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3383"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think maybe it's important to say—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3384"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But it's actually just really hard to get reasoning to that level. It's like vastly above—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3386"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think it's important to say that here there's a question of, like, will we be able to model a verifier with a sufficiently interesting class of errors in theory to approximate the situation? And so I guess the claim is that I think we have a hope of doing that, and I think that includes verifiers that are not just, you take all the questions and you Bernoulli-noise some of them by 90% or something.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3411"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But your fundamental problem is that a majority of your verifiers agree on the wrong answer and continue to do that even after being exposed to debate by a bunch of suggesters.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3422"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Oh, but that's not the way it works. You don't use that—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3424"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But that is the way it works in real life.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3426"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But that's not the way that scalable oversight works. You don't take the top level question and then have a bunch of communication with the verifier, and then at the end, the verifier decides the top level question. The verifier is an input to a process. The machines and the protocol spits out the answer at the end, and so it's running in a way which is not the same as the process you just described.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3452"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Okay. So I just don't think you can do that in real life with real life questions.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Where exactly does the scheme break</span></h2><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3456"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But so let's dig into where it's going wrong. So let's say we zoom forward three years, or say you succeed at governance and we get 10 years or 5 years or something, and we zoom forward 5 years, and we've built a scalable oversight protocol which we think is sufficiently doubly efficient and covers a sufficiently interesting class of verifier with errors.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So is it that you think that will fail because the verifier with errors model is insufficiently rich, or that the proof will be wrong, or that it won't, like, model the heuristics right? I think if there's some, it's good to pin down which part of the step is breaking.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3497"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> It sounds sort of like which part of the perpetual motion machine doesn't work. Like, how are you— extracting correct verdicts out of broken verifiers?</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3506"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I… But at no point in the schemes I'm imagining does the verifier—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3511"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So basically, one of the things you could be postulating is we're going to figure out how to break down reasoning steps to the point where we can just tell whether or not each step of the reasoning is valid. And in this case, you have no need for humans at all, right? What are the humans even doing in this process? Just run the thing that spits out the aligned, truthful, valid reasoning.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3532"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So you wouldn't actually know which of the steps are valid in this picture. You're doing some robust statistical estimation process where—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3539"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Okay, but— Like, robust to bias or robust to noise?</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3543"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Both. You have to model both.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3545"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> How is it— What does anything ever look like that is robust to bias? Why are you just not screwed if the verifiers are biased?</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3552"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think that there are, in fact, systems, like if you have a sufficiently coupled structure of all the questions, you could see how questions relate.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3560"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Is that because we're assuming that out of three ways of rephrasing the question two elicit the correct answer from verifiers?</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3569"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> No, that was exactly the thing I said is not what I mean.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3571"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Okay. But how can this possibly work? Not even the details. What's the grand scheme? What's the insight? What am I missing when I think that this sounds like trying to extract thermodynamic work from a heat bath?</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3584"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think that I do have more faith than you that if you not just paraphrase, but extremely, like, expand each node into a bunch of redundancy and you just elaborate it to death, then you can get to… You can go from a situation where humans are wrong way too often and in a biased way on the raw questions, and you expand them a whole bunch into great detail, and then we are right kind of enough of the time if you look at this kind of coupling structure.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So there's some relationship between— kind of fixing the, doing the error correction and doing the expansion, which I think is important.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What a verifier needs that a coin lacks</span></h2><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3623"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span know.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3630"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think there's a lot more actual information about the world in the humans than in the coin.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3648"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Hey, do you mind talking more into your microphone?</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3676"><b><span style="white-space: question, and I thought, well, that means we need to hire cognitive scientists because it feels like… Initially, I was like, well, the boundary feels like it should be somewhere, probably on priors not that close to humans, either very far away from humans or very far below humans.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3716"><b><span style="white-space: href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3717"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> And then… What?</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3719"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think it's very far above humans.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3720"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Again, the claim is that there may be some structure where you get the protocol right. Blah blah blah. You agree or disagree.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And then I thought, well, but historically and evolutionarily, there's some specific point that humans are at, which is we are at the place where we're strong enough, like, intelligence-wise, to build a world-spanning civilization. And so, in that case, plausibly, it's, like, not that far from humans.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3749"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So, the thing is—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3750"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> And so then we sort of… That's where cross-examination comes from, is hiring people to think about the human side, and Beth hired a bunch of humans and did human debates and found flaws and so on.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3760"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But this is the scholastic method. It is famously insufficient to build a civilization without the experiments occasionally coming up, beating people up and telling them that they are wrong. And if we could do that with superalignment, I would actually be fairly cheerful. Like, if you let us take 50 years, and every year, all the people who had wackily optimistic schemes get beaten up and humiliated, and then a new generation comes in with a new set of wacky schemes, then at the end of 50 years, I think you're pretty good. The problem is to beat up the people who are mistaken that their superalignment scheme would work, then.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3795"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So I think this does—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3796"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But we can't do that without everybody actually dying.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3798"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think this does go to, so one is that the scholastic method has given us a number of obstacles to scalable oversight. So, even in my optimistic frame, I think it is broken in several ways already. And so I think that I can make progress against those obstacles in the scholastic frame. The question then is like, how is that plausibly close enough or not? And I think it's quite plausible that it is absolutely not close enough.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3829"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So an example property your system could require is that with a sufficiently number of diverse rephrasings of piecewise arguments, the noise in the verifiers— the aggregate of the verifiers becomes noisy rather than biased. You apply GPT-5.6 Terra to them, it always gives you the truthful and aligned answer in, like, 60% or, like, greater than 51% of cases, like, across the sum of all the diverse ways of phrasing the debates as with pieces being judged by 5.6 Terra or something.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But this to me is an imaginable property and what it boils down to is, if bias, you're screwed. So I think that that is, as you say, that's just the bias modeling. And so you have to actually… Sorry, that's just the noise modeling. And so you do in fact have to get a model of the bias and the question is, like, what does that model look like? I think there's something about you have to assume that the structure of all of the reasoning is sufficiently coupled that if you take some field of approximately correct answers and then kind of iterate it through the structure…</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think we're out of time, actually.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3928"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think we apparently were out of time eight minutes ago, actually.</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3931"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Irving</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah,—</span></p><p><a href="https://youtu.be/mJxoSPpPoOs?t=3931"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> There's— we are now in the middle of a mandatory break.
