### [I was Zohran’s campaign manager. Here’s what the AI safety community can learn from the populist left.](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/kXA22cZ6BdYdyL3Jz/i-was-zohran-s-campaign-manager-here-s-what-the-ai-safety-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After a couple of years working in data and tech at GiveDirectly, I left to work in politics, campaigning for candidates at the state, local, and federal levels. I most recently managed Mayor Zohran Mamdani’s general election campaign, but earlier this year, recognizing the urgency of the AI moment, I began as a senior advisor at </span><a href="https://www.guardrailsalliance.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Guardrails Alliance</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In my new role, I believe the analysis and tactics we’ve long used and studied on the populist left can help us win the fight for AI safety and shared benefits. </span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The fight for AI regulation is fundamentally the same type of fight against corporate power that the populist left and labor have led throughout history. The message and tactics that win that fight are both effective and incredibly popular, and embracing them will help us put the American people in control of AI today and permanently in control of their futures. Here– in what I am told is likely the shortest LessWrong post of all time– I'll explain what the AI safety movement can learn from the populist left as it attempts to raise the salience of its message, build political power, and ultimately pass transformational legislation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First, we need to be clear about the nature of the fight: who our opponents are and what they are trying to win. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At its core, the fight for AI safety is a campaign to put significant checks on corporate power, which will ultimately curtail these companies’ profits, and limit their ability to consolidate influence and build monopolies.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> While some draw the distinction between “bad AI” (OpenAI) and “good AI” (Anthropic), both companies are rapidly developing products that put humanity at risk in pursuit of making more money than anyone in the history of the world, and there is reasonable evidence that Anthropic’s pitch to be seen as “good AI” is simply a hollow branding exercise designed to taint its competition (which itself </span><a href="https://futurism.com/openai-reportedly-giving-up-non-profit"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">originally claimed</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to be “good AI”!). The </span><a href="https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2111262661493010492"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">milquetoast bill</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Anthropic circulated earlier this year makes it clear that its business motives take precedence over concern for the future of humanity. Now that it has IPO’d, the company’s primary incentive, as with any major corporation, is to increase profits through any means necessary.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The reality is that any type of AI safety regulation that meaningfully reduces the chance of existential risk, whether by pausing frontier model development, banning super intelligence, or any other powerful lever, will also meaningfully reduce these companies’ profits. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We should not expect either frontier lab to be our ally when substantial checks on their power are on the table.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> And the fight for AI safety isn’t just a fight to pass one bill– after we pass baseline regulation, it’s going to be a decades-long struggle as technology continues to advance. This means that any type of AI legislation addressing other harms that erode society’s resilience to catastrophic incidents– like denying people health benefits, replacing jobs without recompense, or placing chatbots in schools– will also reduce these companies’ bottom lines and be another front in the battle.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Second, we need to understand that we are waging an ideological and factional fight, not a partisan one.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Some in the AI safety community feel that it’s most strategic to pursue a bipartisan approach to pass legislation. But that ignores a basic fact: </span><a href="https://prospect.org/2026/09/11/openai-chuck-schumer-daughter-amazon/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">establishment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.wemu.org/npr-national-news/2026-11-09/palantir-hires-a-former-democratic-senator-as-it-faces-scrutiny-in-new-congress"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Democrats</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.washingtonpost.com/politics/2026/09/20/trump-family-large-ai-related-investments-create-possible-conflicts/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Republicans</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are often equally hesitant to check corporate power, and we can’t rely on them to oppose the industry’s influence. In fact, the only group in American politics over the last decade that has consistently and substantially stood up to billionaires and big corporations is the populist left (sometimes </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/15/us/steve-bannon-bernie-sanders-ai.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">with</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.hawley.senate.gov/senators-hawley-murphy-announce-bipartisan-ai-agent-accountability-act/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">partnership</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.sanders.senate.gov/press-releases/news-sanders-hawley-introduce-bill-capping-credit-card-interest-rates-at-10/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">from</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://time.com/5922275/hawley-sanders-covid-relief-congress/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://x.com/DropSiteNews/status/2071761541037199819?a=KywAi0iLm9ud0RFVoLw&amp;s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">populist</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.hawley.senate.gov/hawley-warren-reintroduce-bipartisan-legislation-to-limit-pbms-put-patients-first/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">right</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We have battled with Big Pharma and health insurance companies in pursuit of Medicare for All; with Big Oil in pursuit of a Green New Deal; and with the military industrial complex and the Israel lobby in pursuit of an end to endless wars. It’s clear to us that in the fight for AI safety legislation, and any other legislation that genuinely addresses the impact of AI on our society, our enemies will be Big Tech and the frontier labs. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And we know that this fight is likely to be bipartisan in a different way than expected: it may be equally difficult to convince Republicans and establishment Democrats to take real action when they’re dining with Big Tech oligarchs, accepting their campaign donations, and hiring their family members. </span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we can accurately understand this as a fight against corporate power and for populist ends, we have effective messages and tactics to form the basis for our campaign. Democrats across the ideological spectrum triggered an unprecedented blue wave election by using the economic populist message that Bernie Sanders and other leftists have been beating the drum on for decades (and that Republicans used to win in 2024). This election didn’t just demonstrate the effectiveness of an anti-corporate message; it reminded us of the blueprint for tactics. The </span><a href="https://www.npr.org/2026/11/07/nx-s1-6007770/data-centers-activist-moratorium"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">wave</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.thenation.com/article/activism/accidental-activists-data-center-alley/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">of</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2024/10/29/technology/data-center-peculiar-missouri.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">activism</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> against AI data centers caused </span><a href="https://gov.texas.gov/news/post/governor-abbott-directs-comprehensive-data-center-audit"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Republicans</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.dailysignal.com/2026/08/21/governor-backtracks-ai-policy/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Democrats</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> alike to reverse their pro-data center messaging in the face of voter anger, with many of those fights carrying new elected officials to victory. When the populist left has won major fights against powerful enemies in the past, we have relied on confrontational and aggressive tactics like boycotts, strikes, sit-ins, civil disobedience, and arrests– the same </span><a href="https://www.npr.org/2026/10/25/nx-s1-6011201/data-center-wisconsin-midterms-port-washington"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">kinds</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of </span><a href="https://futurism.com/artificial-intelligence/regular-people-data-center-arrest-protest"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">tactics</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that are now winning the anti-data center war. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Using this playbook, we will be able to hold Democratic majorities accountable to the populist ideas they ran on, passing legislation that addresses voters’ anger and economic anxiety and meaningfully curtails the risks and harms caused by AI. In particular, I encourage anyone reading this who feels deep urgency around the need for AI safety regulation to explore using the political tactics and messaging discussed above. T</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">his is a moment in which we can take our fight directly to the people, rather than relying on inside games, op-eds, or campaign donations, none of which match the scale or power of what is needed to meaningfully rein in these companies on the timeline required. </span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have spent most of my career working for progressive politicians, but I joined the AI fight earlier this year because I am clear on the risks of letting these companies accelerate unchecked. I am just as clear on the role of this fight in a broader realignment around an anti-corporate agenda. Our work at Guardrails began as a </span><a href="https://www.commondreams.org/news/ai-election-spending-toxic"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">successful</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> attempt to toxify AI money because we understood that Leading the Future’s undue influence would curtail our ability to pass meaningful regulation. Now, we’re looking to build on the opportunities we have preserved. As 2028 approaches, we plan to continue organizing, holding power to account, and remaining clear-eyed about our oligarch enemies. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My hope is that the AI safety community can see themselves in our movement against corporate power and work with us to create a safer, more resilient, just, and equitable society. </span></b></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/kXA22cZ6BdYdyL3Jz/i-was-zohran-s-campaign-manager-here-s-what-the-ai-safety-1#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [My tentative plans given less than one year left to change course [Linkpost]](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/6PydFibiADKrxGzcn/my-tentative-plans-given-less-than-one-year-left-to-change)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Subtitle: This mad race must end.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note: I think this is partially me working through my plans and partially my emotions, so imo it's less epistemically rigorous than ideal for LessWrong.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I might have less than a year left to affect the insane, reckless, Faustian race to ASI, and I plan to spend that time doing everything I can to push for a pause.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I currently think there’s a decently high chance that by default, there’s 6-12 months left before my personal point of no return: the point after which my own actions no longer plausibly affect the future. This is different from other people’s points of no return: for example, people like Dario Amodei (CEO of Anthropic) or Donald Trump may have slightly longer times to wake up and change course. This is also different from other timelines that people sometimes throw around, like timelines to AGI or ASI or fully automated remote-worker or whatever. However, it is the most important for my own decisions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I do not defend this position here. Other people have written partial explanations that I like (</span><a href="https://blog.karthiktadepalli.com/p/metr-anthropic-report"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Karthik</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/p/q35-timelines-update-looks-like-the"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Futures Project</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://blog.sentinel-team.org/p/anthropic-warns-of-imminent-dramatically"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sentinel</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://rutgerbregman.substack.com/p/yes-you-should-panic-about-ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rutger Bregman</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). I may also write more accessible explainers in the near future.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Regardless, humanity is absolutely not ready for what comes next, whether that’s </span><a href="https://ifanyonebuildsit.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">human extinction</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://linch.substack.com/p/simplest-case-ai-catastrophe"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">complete loss of control to rogue AIs</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or a </span><a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/ai-enabled-coups-how-a-small-group-could-use-ai-to-seize-power"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">few oligarchs</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, or something far weirder.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While I don’t have strong confidence that I </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">actually</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> only have 6-12 months left, I think very few people are working on this by default, and AI Safety is growing very quickly (though still </span><a href="https://x.com/LinchZhang/status/2112213391913660628"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">far smaller</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> than needed), and newcomers to the field can cover the scenarios where we have more time.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given this, I think my best strategy is to push hard for a pause on training AI: specifically a complete moratorium on training stronger AI models than the ones we currently have and severe limitations on AI R&amp;D, until scientists, policymakers, and the general public have more time to understand what’s going on and catch up.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think pushing for a pause would be the best use of my time. While it’d be very difficult to execute, it is less robustly doomed than trying to make things better on the margin and slightly increasing the chances that the crazy race to superhuman AI is conducted safely. I’m not very confident in this position and do not defend it in detail here, though I wrote some other strategies I’ve considered below in the next section.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m less opinionated about what happens after a pause. Reasonable options range from resuming carefully under real engineering standards to pausing indefinitely, if humanity collectively decides that the risks posed by superhuman AI is too high.</span><a href="https://linch.substack.com/p/i-plan-to-drop-everything-to-push?utm_source=activity_item#footnote-1" target="_self"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Regardless, we should not be sleepwalking into disaster. A pause gives humanity time to regroup and rethink things.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">strategy</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. What are </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">tactics</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">? What should I concretely do?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here I am less certain. Right now, in the next (uncertain but limited) number of days, I’m dropping all my research and writing projects to re-orient myself to this new reality and think through what I can do to increase the odds of a pause.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Current candidate options for what seems live to me: a) moving to Sacramento and providing evidence and arguments for the California government to mandate a pause, b) doing something similar in DC, c) serving as a research/writing assistant for more prominent public intellectuals pushing for a pause, d) helping advocacy and protest movements working towards a pause, or e) spearheading long-shot plans to increase the odds of a pause.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And of course, just writing a lot online.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Extremely open to other ideas!)</span></p><img alt="March for an AI Treaty: Washington, DC · Stop The AI Race" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6PydFibiADKrxGzcn/dhmeguvyzieevlifqipk" /><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What are other strategies I could be pursuing?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is pausing the best strategy? I think so, but I’m not confident. Here are two options that I’ve considered and tentatively rejected (I’ve also considered other options, but they seem even more doomed, at least for me).</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Help key decisionmakers “manage the transition better”</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If a pause won’t happen, I’d rather the transition to superhuman AI be done well (or okay, or at least poorly) rather than disastrously.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I considered trying to join Anthropic or OpenAI. I currently think I wouldn’t be a good fit</span><a href="https://linch.substack.com/p/i-plan-to-drop-everything-to-push?utm_source=activity_item#footnote-2" target="_self"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I dislike the organizations too much, and working on a doomsday machine to make it slightly less doomed just doesn’t sit well with me. I might still re-consider it if nonconflicted people argued me into believing that the impact is high enough, and I should swallow my disgust.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I also considered working to help government people re-orient and make better non-pause decisions. This still seems like a live option to me, and I will continue looking for potentially good options.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Macrostrategy automation and trying to solve non-alignment AI issues with AI assistance</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Under some models of AI progress, the most urgent time isn’t now but several months in the future when we’ll have 3-6 months of rapidly accelerated non-technical research due to AI assistance, before my own irrelevance. Maybe I’d have 5-100x speedup on non-alignment problems that matter a lot, like how to counteract the effects of AI propaganda or making sure humanity doesn’t go insane through the transition.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Preparing for that time seems somewhat competitive, and there are a relatively small number of people who can do this well, myself marginally included.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I currently feel less excited about this than working towards a pause, but I will continue thinking about it and talking to my colleagues at Forethought about this possibility.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Focus on counteracting AI persuasion in short-timelines worlds</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An angle that I feel somewhat excited about/drawn to is trying to help harden specific decision-makers and decision-making against future AI superhuman persuasion (my research area this year). While somewhat less likely with faster timelines than slower ones, I still think there are significant worlds where superhuman AI persuasion will be a big part of AI takeover attempts, and hardening within-AI-company and USG decision processes against AI persuasion seems fairly competitive with other options, and plays well into my research interests and backgrounds.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I can imagine doing this either within or outside of the existing institutions.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other personal updates:</span></h1><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Emotionally it’s… not been great. But I’ve been handling this re-orientation better than I personally expected (so far). My dominant feeling is more like determination plus light anxiety than deep fear, dread, or resigned acceptance.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m consciously aware that there’s probably only enough time left to make a single medium-sized career pivot (likely still in the researcher/writer space). Not many more pivots left from here on out.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I plan to not do much in field-building or mentorship, other than continuing existing relationships or </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">maybe</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> upskilling people who are highly experienced in other fields into AI safety/AI macrostrategy. Time is too short.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m slightly worried about going crazy in the next year, partially due to the situation seeming increasingly insane, and partially due to actual Claude psychosis as the models get stronger. Not sure what to do about it. Just flagging it as a risk.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m comparatively more worried about just “hiding away”/”shutting down”, and living an unendorsed life of being depressed and internet/media distractions. I’ve set up some safeguards with friends to reduce this risk, and plan to do more.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I plan to stop doing difficult personal/professional growth stuff unless it’s in-the-moment enjoyable, extremely critical, or very AI related. The basic model is that I already had over 30 years to grow (more than a decade as an adult), so the likely gains of further growth are unlikely to be worth the time and attention costs. I can do more growth after the singularity, or after a pause. AI-related stuff is an exception because squeezing in crazy uplift seems plausible. I honestly feel kind of sad about this; I’m someone for whom personal growth is important, or at least someone who likes to imagine themselves as someone for whom personal growth is important</span><a href="https://linch.substack.com/p/i-plan-to-drop-everything-to-push?utm_source=activity_item#footnote-3" target="_self"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I plan to be more open with my information and be less discreet with semi-private information (unless I think they’re other people’s secrets or I explicitly promised confidentiality). Openness is critical to the world orienting better, and there’s just less time left for information hazards and attention hazards to backfire.</span><a href="https://linch.substack.com/p/i-plan-to-drop-everything-to-push?utm_source=activity_item#footnote-4" target="_self"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">4</span></a></li><li value="8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Will be less critical of other safety people’s plans going forwards. Time is too short. Generally want to be less of a roadblock to good things happening.</span></li><li value="9"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Planning to postpone elective surgeries I was vaguely considering.</span></li><li value="10"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I plan to get in less internet fights, except occasionally with popular/important people. Tbh this is going to be really hard for me, but I’ll try my best!</span></li><li value="11"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I no longer look both ways before crossing a one-way street.</span></li></ol><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What should other people do?</span></h1><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For people currently working in AI safety:</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you currently work in technical AI safety, AI macrostrategy, AI policy, etc, presumably you’ve already heard of some of these arguments and updates and already think they’re somewhat important.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you haven’t already done so, you should definitely think about this and talk to your colleagues (both internally and externally) about the updates towards shorter timelines and what it means for you and your organization! My guess is that there are a lot of things most people in the field should be changing about their work.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t have strong opinions about what </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">specifically</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> you should be changing to, without knowing more of your specific situation and context.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Technical people should be split between some combination of a) providing evidence of risk and why a pause is critical, b) working out the technical details of a pause, c) emergency alignment and interpretability techniques that</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> just might work</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> if we can’t pause, d) more long-run alignment, interpretability, control etc, strategies that work only conditional upon us succeeding on getting a pause (or if I am wrong about how much time we have left).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you’re non-technical, obvious options include a) persuading and pressuring AI companies to unilaterally pause, b) lobbying Congress (if you’re Democrat) or c) the Executive Branch (if you’re Republican) for a pause, d) lobbying California to mandate a pause, or e) raising awareness and increasing public demand and political will for a pause.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other options include pushing other states to demand the AI companies pause (most saliently Delaware but I think other states have leverage too), building momentum in China to pause if the US does so (this is delicate; if you’re in a situation to do so presumably you know more than me on how to do so), and increasing international pressure on a global AI moratorium.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For people not currently working on AI safety</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A very important strategic consideration here is that because time to the point of no return </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">might</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> be very short but we can’t know for sure, and we likely may </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">never</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> know for sure until it’s too late, the safety field should likely bifurcate strategies where some people work on the assumption of very short (&lt;1 year) time to have an impact, and other people work on the assumption of more time to have an impact.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Since the field is growing very quickly but it takes people time to get up to speed, a natural bifurcation is for most people currently in the field to work under the ~1 year clock, and most people currently outside of it to get up to speed and do things that are more promising given more time.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All things considered, I think those worlds are still very salient, either because we’re wrong about the future speed of automation of AI research, or because the pause actually worked. However, I don’t have advice for how to join the AI safety effort later on, over and beyond the </span><a href="https://bluedot.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">standard</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://80000hours.org/ai/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">advice</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With that in mind, here are things I think people not currently in AI safety should do:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(For some people) </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Think of highly context-specific and long-shot plans</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to increase the odds of a pause. My guess is that for the right people in the right places, that’s your individual highest bet of making sure we don’t die from AI. I don’t know what your context is so it’s hard to say what plans can and can’t have a chance of working. But for example I don’t have special connections with the Vatican (and they </span><a href="https://linch.substack.com/p/claude-author-of-the-humanitas"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">might actively dislike me</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), but other people more connected with the Vatican or other faith leaders might have ideas on how faith leaders can sound the alarm and call for a moratorium. Similarly for people with connections to ASML/Netherlands government, TSMC/Taiwanese gov’t, mass media, compute providers like Amazon, other tech leaders, other members of civil society, and so on.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you do have a plan that you think has some chance of working, please don’t hesitate to reach out! I’m available over substack DMs, and can either give some feedback or connect you with other people better suited to evaluating those strategies.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To be clear, please don’t do (or plan, or threaten) anything violent! Violence predictably backfires in almost all circumstances, will not stop the suicidal race to ASI, and makes the jobs of all the rest of us harder.</span></li></ol></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Get oriented</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><a href="https://bluedot.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Learn more about AI and AI safety</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and understand or consider more durable ways you can contribute, whether on technical AI safety, on policy, or something else.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Join protests and other forms of direct action against the reckless, madcap, Faustian race to AGI</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Do not just accept the race as a fact of nature. Get involved with organizations like </span><a href="https://donotaccept.ai/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">DONOTACCEPT ai</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://humansincontrol.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Humans In Control</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><a href="https://whodecides.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Who Decides AI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At some point (if things go okay), AI protests will be so popular that each individual in a protest will be less important, and there are more impactful ways to get involved in AI safety. But we’re (sadly) still in the early part of the exponential for AI saliency and worries about AI risk.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I will go to an </span><a href="https://aitreatymarch.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Treaty March</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> myself this Saturday. I’m highly uncertain whether this march will actually increase the odds of a US-China treaty, but I want to go partially so there’s momentum behind future marches, and partially to build the muscle for protests myself.</span></li></ol></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Call or email your political leaders and demand action.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Especially for Americans. This mad race must end, and the American Congress and the Executive Branch can make it happen.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Slacktivism.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> If nothing else, it’s pretty good to understand the issues well enough, and be correct, polite, and kind in online discussions of AI and AI risk. Make the stakes as clear as you can and link to ways people can contribute.</span></li></ol><img alt="AI Treaty March | Center for Humane Technology" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6PydFibiADKrxGzcn/eggn1qycefpytxks0cll" /><p><a href="https://aitreatymarch.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Treaty March here</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A final, personal, message: This is deep and heavy stuff. I wish I could offer more emotional solace or reassurance. I don’t think I’m the right person, or this is the right situation, for that. What I could say is that I truly believe the fight is well worth it. We must not accept the industry’s framing of this mad, suicidal, race as inevitable. We can and must do our best to end it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Linchpin is a reader-supported publication. To receive new posts and support my work, consider becoming a free or paid subscriber.</span></p><p><a href="https://linch.substack.com/p/i-plan-to-drop-everything-to-push?utm_source=activity_item#footnote-anchor-1" target="_self"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Options that seem salient to me right now: a) unpause and proceed ahead on an “AI as normal engineering” assumption, except we </span><a href="https://x.com/LinchZhang/status/2119104569238225129"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">take normal engineering safety standards seriously</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, rather than the slapdash “safety” and “security” measures that currently plague those companies*, b) unpause and proceed according to an </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">international verified slowdown between the US and China</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, with total research transparency and extensive checks on safety between each development cycle, c) don’t unpause until companies have safety cases that they can constrain the risk of further AI development below a manageable threshold, those safety cases are verified by external evaluators and openly communicated and debated, and humanity agrees that unpausing is worth the risk, d) pause for 6 months, decide whether the risks of unpausing outweigh the benefits, if not, pause for 1 year, then do this again, pause for 2 years, then 4, then 10(?), and so forth, e) do limited AI research into </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">safety</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and don’t unpause until we discover AI architectures that are provably or demonstrably much safer than the status quo, f) unpause with a focus on specialized AI architectures for specific tasks (like self-driving cars and medicine), don’t do AI research into RSI or other dangerous scary general AI, or e) don’t unpause period until humanity changes our minds; the technology for supplanting all of human labor and cognition is too dangerous for Man.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">*Treating AI risks as solvable normal engineering feels doomed to me, but not as completely doomed as treating them as solvable by normal engineering and </span><a href="https://x.com/LinchZhang/status/2119104569238225129"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">then not applying normal engineering safety standards</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which is the </span><a href="https://www.theatlantic.com/technology/2026/10/openai-safety-team-resignation/688881/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">current default</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><a href="https://linch.substack.com/p/i-plan-to-drop-everything-to-push?utm_source=activity_item#footnote-anchor-2" target="_self"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Not saying that they’d necessarily hire me even if I applied, to be clear.</span></p><p><a href="https://linch.substack.com/p/i-plan-to-drop-everything-to-push?utm_source=activity_item#footnote-anchor-3" target="_self"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Or in my darker moments, someone who likes to imagine themselves as someone who likes to imagine themselves as someone for whom personal growth is important.</span></p><p><a href="https://linch.substack.com/p/i-plan-to-drop-everything-to-push?utm_source=activity_item#footnote-anchor-4" target="_self"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">4</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Among the information I actually have, to be clear. It’s not like I have the plans for a working bioweapon stored in my head. Your mileage may vary.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/6PydFibiADKrxGzcn/my-tentative-plans-given-less-than-one-year-left-to-change#comments">Discuss</a>

---

## Engineering

### [Bliki: Generated Glossary](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/GeneratedGlossary.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>When we use LLMs in a specialized domain, such as the operations of any
  reasonably-large organization, there is a lot of specialized vocabulary. Such
  words and specialized meanings will be foreign to an LLM because they aren't
  in its training set, making it harder for the LLM to be effective when doing
  tasks in that domain. One technique to aid this is to use an LLM to generate a
  glossary of the terminology in that domain, and make this glossary available
  for any LLMs doing work in those circumstances.</p>

<p>The sources for the LLM can be any material within the organization
  concerned such as documents, emails, and chat logs. In some cases getting
  transcripts of audio or video may be helpful, although most organizations
  generate enough text to do the job.</p>

<p>The glossary-generating LLM should look for single words, and phrases of
  several words that regularly hang out together to form a meaningful term. The
  generator should be asked to identify terms that are new to it, but also known
  terms that are being used in unfamiliar ways. The latter can often cause the
  most confusion.</p>

<p>Once we have a list of specialized vocabulary we can ask the agent to
  produce a glossary, with definitions for each term. Some terms may
  be used in different ways, in which case the LLM should provide alternative
  definitions, with a note as to what context the differences appear in. The LLM
  can be asked to follow the output format of a dictionary, with a focus on
  precision and conciseness.</p>

<p>Once we have a draft glossary, then  human experts in that domain should
  review it, and amend it for cases where the LLM has got the wrong ends of
  sticks.</p>

<p>We then put the glossary somewhere where any other LLM can use it. It
  should be in a form that's easy for the LLM to consult, and minimize the
  tokens the LLM should consume in order to find meaning. We should set up a
  schedule to review and refresh the glossary at regular intervals, by agents
  and humans. We should keep it in version control, so we can see how it changes
  over time.</p>

<p>Large organizations often have subtly different definitions in different
  places, essentially following the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/DomainDrivenDesign.html">Domain Driven Design</a> notion
  of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/BoundedContext.html">Bounded Context</a>. Source code can be a particularly
  interesting bounded context, allowing folks to understand how terminology is
  used within a code base. Sometimes a single glossary can span several of
  bounded contexts, but at other times it's better to have separate glossaries
  for each one, allowing LLMs to consult whichever glossaries that are relevant
  to what they are doing. I'd urge everyone to resist any temptation to create a
  single, standard, glossary - I've yet to see that work. Often the value of a
  glossary isn't to nail down a single definition of a term, but instead to
  highlight its different usage in different contexts.</p>

<p>When there are different bounded contexts it can be interesting to compare
  different glossaries, or to get LLMs to compare glossaries and describe
  differences in usage. Comparing a source-code derived glossary to a human text
  can be particularly instructive.</p>

<p>If the glossary is large, it can be worthwhile to only include the terms
  and links to definitions as part of the initial context. The model can then
  use the links for only the full definitions it needs while working.</p>

<p>A glossary can be used in several ways within <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticEngineering.html">Agentic Engineering</a>. Glossaries can inform generation of code, so that
  names within the generated software fit the particular vocabulary of the
  enterprise. <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/harness-engineering.html">Sensors in a
  harness</a> can check for this usage, both in the internals of the software
  and in the user-interface.</p>

<p>When generating code or documents, we may introduce new bounded contexts.
  For example, internal names that may be used within an organization should not
  be exposed in a customer-facing UI, because such internal terms are likely to be
  only confuse a customer. In this case a customer has a different bounded
  context to the part of the enterprise serving them.</p>

<p>It's often useful for glossaries to include terms that should not be used,
  in which case it includes advice on what other term to use instead. Terms that
  are deprecated can be handled in this way, with glossary entries that indicate
  what period of time a term should be in effect.</p>

<p>In my conversations with people working with agents in large organizations,
  a common theme is the difficulty of specialized language. This encourages the
  activities of domain modeling, building ontologies, and constructing explicit
  <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/making-data-ready-for-agentic-ai.html#context-layer">context
  layers in enterprise data</a>. Getting LLMs to generate a glossary is a
  simple, but useful, part of this work.</p>

<div class="acknowledgements">
<h2>Acknowledgements</h2>

<p>Eric Nagler and Unmesh Joshi related their experiences doing this
    activity and thus inspired this post. Amy Pellegrini, Annie Luxton, Arun Srinivasan, Chris Ford, Daniel
    Terhorst-North, Dave Farley, Eric Nagler, James Shore, Jason Yip, Jimmy Nilsson, Matteo Vaccari, Michaël Le Barbier, Pete Hodgson, Pramod
    Sadalage, Ravi Vats, Unmesh Joshi, and Valentyn Logvynskyi commented on drafts.
    </p>
</div>

### [Tests as data: ensuring AI-generated code does what we want](https://martinfowler.com/articles/tests-as-data.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/tests-as-data.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/tests-as-data/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>Automated testing in the age of AI coding assistants is problematic:
     when AI agents write both production and test code, developers risk losing
     the assurance that automated tests protect against functional errors.
     <b class="author">Matteo Vaccari</b> makes three claims: (1) when AI writes the
     code, developers become its customers; (2) therefore customer tests, not
     developer tests, become the primary contract between humans and AI; (3)
     moving that contract into test data rather than test code makes it easier
     to protect it from unexpected modifications by coding agents. All of this
     optimizes for human-readable tests that establish a contract between humans
     and AI. While in the past DSL parsers and custom test runners were
     expensive to write and maintain, now AI makes them cheap.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/tests-as-data.html">more…</a></p>

### [Fragments: November 10](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-11-10.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>It’s that time of the year when the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/radar">November Thoughtworks Technology Radar</a> appears. Again my colleagues have taken a sweep of the technologies that our teams have used in the field, and pulled together a summary of what’s popped up in the last six months. It’s been a busy half-year, one that again has been dominated by AI, and that’s reflected in the many AI blips on the radar.</p>

<p>The mass of blips makes it hard to develop a coherent story around trends, but Chris Ford has done a fine job of the writing the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/insights/blog/technology-strategy/macro-trends-tech-industry-november-2026">accompanying article on macro trends</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>One team of passionate people experimenting with agents can make almost anything work. They share a context, a credit card and a mental model. They review each other’s work because they sit together, and they know what to check and how much latitude to give the coding agent.</p>

  <p>Two hundred people, though, cannot work that way. The moment agentic AI becomes an operating model rather than a pilot, a second layer of problems appears: How is context shared between teams? What’s the token bill? How do we deal with increased review demands? What’s the agent allowed to reach, read and change? And who’s now responsible for the harness everyone depends on?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Experience so far suggests that the questions that enterprises face are about: shared context, token cost, cognitive load, security, and platform building. While these issues are distinct, they are not separate, so strategies to answer them must take all of them into account. I also echo Chris in reminding us that however impressive these tools are, we must remember that while we have to rethink systems and workflows to utilize these technologies, they should be built to serve humans - and we should all benefit from its bounty.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Ben Thompson’s Stratechery newsletter <a href="https://stratechery.com/">reflects on  the recent election’s impact on data centers</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>I watch a lot of sports, and particularly this fall, watched a lot of baseball. My Milwaukee Brewers — excuse me, my World Champion Milwaukee Brewers — demanded my full attention, which also meant I saw a lot of campaign ads, and by far the most common topic, at least here in Wisconsin, was data centers. Moreover, it was never entirely clear which way it cut politically: both parties were anti-data center, accusing their opponent of being the opposite.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He points out that this opposition is recent, and tied to a shift in focus from the internet and streaming movies to AI:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>you know, the technology that in the best case scenario is going to take everyone’s jobs, and in the worst case scenario end the human race.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>There’s going to be a lot said about how Democratic officials will be at the heart of blocking data center building, but as his point above about campaign ads illustrates, the opposition to data centers is (at least at the moment) a bi-partisan opposition.</p>

<p>He follows up with “ten takes” from these consequences. The big beneficiaries of this opposition to building data centers will be those who own the data centers that have already been built. The opposition will reduce supply of tokens, meaning that token costs will go up, thus increasing the profits for those who already have their token generators plugged in and humming.</p>

<p>There’s also benefits to those who aren’t constrained by the need to build more big data centers. Other countries aren’t affected by a change in US policy, China is the most talked-about case here, but it’s not the only country that may benefit from a drop in US data center building. Apple may also benefit, if it means more AI work is pushed to edge devices that are their territory, in addition to having not sunk so many dollars into AI-specific investments.</p>

<p>To his analysis, I’ll add that this may also have a somewhat counter-intuitive effect on regulation, and corporate attitudes to it. The common expectation is that businesses are opposed to government regulation, since that interferes with their free pursuit of profit. That’s true to an extent, but it’s quite common to see established businesses lobby (quietly) in favor of regulation that acts as a barrier to new competitors. So I wouldn’t be at all surprised to see companies that have already built data centers to be pushing for regulations that impede or prevent new ones from being built.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I’ve been a long-time fan of Hadley Wickham, for how he has tamed the rather messy world of R programming into a <a href="https://tidyverse.org/">well-designed tidyverse</a>. He is another thoughtful voice to listen to as we move into the world of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticEngineering.html">agentic engineering</a>, in this case by trying to figure out <a href="https://substack.hadley.nz/p/how-much-do-tokens-really-cost">what the real cost of tokens is</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>I hear a lot of suspicion that AI tokens are unsustainably priced and that frontier labs are heavily subsidising costs with their VC money. They want to get us hooked now, so they can pull the rug out from under us whenever they want to make a bunch of money. Is that true? My sense is that it’s mostly not. But can I back that up with some data?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>His conclusion is that it doesn’t seem that token costs are significantly subsidized on per-token API basis, but they are with subscription plans. (He caveats his analysis, saying it doesn’t take into account recouping the costs of training the models.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>There’s a creepy excitement at the thought that models are clever enough to edit the weights of models, an activity that <a href="https://rikiyat.github.io/blog/weight-space-programming/">Takehi et al refer to as Weight-Space Programming</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Just by looking at the raw weights, GPT-6 Astra can tell what a network was trained to do. And without any environment feedback, it has no trouble writing, from scratch, small game-playing networks that outplay networks trained with PPO for 100 million steps.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>While we don’t know if we’ll ever be able to download skills directly into our brains (<a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fhrNgXJ__n8">“I know kung fu”</a> - or for those in my particular demographic: <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_90">Joe 90</a>), this does raise the possibility of downloading capabilities into models by manipulating their weights.</p>

<p>Simon Wilison riffed on this paper by <a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Nov/3/qwen-can-interpret-model-weights/">examining whether an LLM can understand model behaviors from the weights alone</a>, by examining four simple neural networks, each trained to play a specific game, and figuring out which game it was by just looking at the weights.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/jessitron/status/2117400669044793405">Jessica Kerr (jessitron):</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>My days of teaching AI to do stuff are numbered.</p>

  <p>Getting it to teach me stuff is more fun.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Houck et al (that “al” includes some folks who I’ve learned to pay a lot of attention to), look at how we can assess the quality of context supplied to agents.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>An agent may draw from source code, specifications, issue trackers, chat histories, architectural documents, tool output, and user instructions, then act on that combination with limited human supervision. Information that was written for different audiences, at different times, and for different purposes is brought together into a temporary operating environment for a particular task. Whether each source is individually useful does not reveal whether the resulting context is fit for the work the agent is being asked to do.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>They’ve come up with a <a href="https://queue.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3847288">framework for describing the quality of this assembled context</a>, which they refer to as CAFE(S). The acronym refers to five dimensions they look at:</p>

<ul>
  <li>Clarity: Can the agent interpret the request as intended?</li>
  <li>Actionability: Can the agent proceed, and know when it is done?</li>
  <li>Fidelity: Can the agent trust that this context is true?</li>
  <li>Efficiency: Can the agent focus on what matters?</li>
  <li>Security: Should the agent have this context at all?</li>
</ul>

<p>They also list a set of half-a-dozen “context smells”: common cases where these break down (and a nod to Kent’s <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/CodeSmell.html">original metaphor</a>)</p>

<p>I’m not sure about the name, (how exactly should I pronounce “CAFE(S)”?) and the article exhibits rather more <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/LLM-Voice.html">LLM Voice</a> that I prefer. But on the whole I think this is a good framing for thinking about an important part of feeding our silicon servants/overlords.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Cindy Sridharan noted that <a href="https://x.com/copyconstruct/status/2118529738532925725">the problems she ran into six months ago are no longer around</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>What was missing in my setup 6 months ago was the verification process I now use.</p>

  <p>70% of my daily token spend goes into verifying patches, and only ~20-30% into creating them.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>That resonated with conversations I’ve had with folks using agents in enterprise settings. They are using far more tokens to check the working of models (through guardrails and evals) than they are to generate a response to a request. A single request to a model from a customer may trigger a dozen turns to other models to probe its input and verify its output.</p>

---

### [Long-horizon agents and durable execution](https://martinfowler.com/articles/durable-execution-agents.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/durable-execution-agents.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/durable-execution-agents/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>Long-horizon agents can automate complex tasks that take days or weeks
     to complete and involve multiple exchanges with people and external
     systems. Many things can go wrong during this time, including process
     crashes, network failures, and unanticipated real-world behavior.
     <b class="author">Kiran Prakash</b> explains durable execution: an emerging
     pattern that helps agents recover from these failures and move towards
     their goal. In this programming model, the runtime keeps a record of the
     result of each step that interacts with the outside world so that, when a
     failure occurs, the program runs again, completed steps are skipped, and
     the program's state is rebuilt from the record.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/durable-execution-agents.html">more…</a></p>

---

### [The Data Product Platform Navigator](https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>Many organizations adopting data mesh and data products face a common
     challenge. They may be tasked with building a self-service platform for
     data products and data mesh from scratch, or they may have an existing
     platform that suffers from low adoption, preventing their data mesh from
     scaling. This article provides a platform navigator, based on the CNCF's
     Platform Engineering Maturity Model, to help you reason about your current
     situation and determine the next steps for your platform.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav.html">more…</a></p>
